    "text": "---\r\nname: \"pdf\"\r\ndescription: \"Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction.\"\r\n---\r\n\r\n# PDF Skill\r\n\r\n## When To Use\r\n\r\n- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.\r\n- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.\r\n- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.\r\n- Validate final rendering before delivery.\r\n\r\n## Tools + Contract Requirements\r\n\r\nImmediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.\r\n\r\n```bash\r\nnode container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf\r\n```\r\n\r\n## Workflow\r\n\r\n1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.\r\n   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.\r\n   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.\r\n2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.\r\n3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.\r\n4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.\r\n\r\n## Fill And Validate AcroForms\r\n\r\nVisual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.\r\n\r\n1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.\r\n2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.\r\n3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:\r\n\r\n```python\r\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\r\nfrom pypdf.generic import NameObject\r\n\r\nreader = PdfReader(input_pdf)\r\nwriter = PdfWriter()\r\nwriter.clone_document_from_reader(reader)\r\n\r\n# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.\r\nwriter.reattach_fields()\r\nfields = writer.get_fields() or {}\r\nmissing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)\r\nif missing:\r\n    raise ValueError(f\"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}\")\r\n\r\nvalues_to_write = dict(expected_values)\r\nif flatten:\r\n    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.\r\n    values_to_write = {\r\n        name: field.get(\"/V\", \"/Off\" if field.get(\"/FT\") == \"/Btn\" else \"\")\r\n        for name, field in fields.items()\r\n    }\r\n    values_to_write.update(expected_values)\r\n\r\nwriter.update_page_form_field_values(\r\n    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten\r\n)\r\n\r\nif flatten:\r\n    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.\r\n    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes=\"/Widget\")\r\n    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject(\"/AcroForm\"), None)\r\n\r\nwith open(output_pdf, \"wb\") as stream:\r\n    writer.write(stream)\r\n```\r\n\r\n4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.\r\n5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.\r\n\r\n## Temp And Output Conventions\r\n\r\n- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.\r\n- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.\r\n- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.\r\n\r\n## Dependencies\r\n\r\nPrefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:\r\n\r\n- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`\r\n- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler\r\n\r\nIf a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.\r\n\r\nPython packages:\r\n\r\n```bash\r\nuv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf\r\n```\r\n\r\nIf `uv` is unavailable:\r\n\r\n```bash\r\npython3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf\r\n```\r\n\r\nSystem tools for rendering:\r\n\r\n```bash\r\n# macOS (Homebrew)\r\nbrew install poppler\r\n\r\n# Ubuntu/Debian\r\nsudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils\r\n```\r\n\r\nIf installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.\r\n\r\n## Environment\r\n\r\nNo required environment variables.\r\n\r\n## Rendering Command\r\n\r\n```bash\r\npdftoppm -png \"$INPUT_PDF\" \"$OUTPUT_PREFIX\"\r\n```\r\n\r\n## Quality Expectations\r\n\r\n- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.\r\n- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.\r\n- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.\r\n- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.\r\n- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.\r\n\r\n## Final Checks\r\n\r\n- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.\r\n- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.\r\n- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.\r\n\r\n## Final response\r\n\r\n### Final response citations\r\n\r\nPlace `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Arango-Martinez}\\par\r\n{\\Large\\bfseries Milestone 1: Proposed Datasets}\\par\r\n{\\normalsize Pressure Prediction in Drinking-Water Distribution Systems}\\par\r\n\\vspace{2pt}\\hrule\\vspace{5pt}\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Area of interest.}\r\nMy interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This builds on my previous work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements. For this project, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure. Both candidates below contain simulated network data and support a comparison of learning methods beyond exploratory analysis.\r\n\r\n\\datasetheading{1. BattLeDIM: L-Town}\r\n\\textbf{Sources:}\r\n\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle dataset}\r\n\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\r\n\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation (Zenodo)}.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Description.}\r\nBattLeDIM was developed to evaluate leakage detection and localization in the simulated L-Town network. It provides measurements for 2018 and 2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 time observations per year, or 210,240 in total. Its 119 measurement channels comprise 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings of consumption. These are numerical time-series variables. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Proposed task.}\r\nPredict pressure at a selected monitored node \\textbf{15 minutes ahead}. Potential inputs include current and past pressures, flows, tank level, consumption, and time information. The target is a continuous pressure value, so this is regression rather than classification. The node would be selected after reviewing network topology and pressure conditions, with preference for a location relevant to critical-node pressure management.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Why consider it?}\r\nIts measurement frequency and distributed sensors closely match my engineering interests, and it has substantially more observations than variables. The original benchmark offers documented prior work on leakage detection; pressure forecasting provides a different project focus. A possible later extension is to examine whether forecast errors change during leakage events.\r\n\r\n\\datasetheading{2. BATADAL: C-Town}\r\n\\textbf{Sources:}\r\n\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle dataset}\r\n\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\r\n\\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation}.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Description.}\r\nBATADAL was developed for cyberattack detection in the simulated C-Town network. The hourly data include 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 records in a second development set, and 2,089 test records: 15,027 in total. Its 43 SCADA variables comprise seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 actuator states, and 12 pressures. Measurements are numerical; actuator states are discrete. Attack information is available separately, with incomplete labeling in the original development data.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Proposed task.}\r\nPredict pressure at a selected monitored network location \\textbf{one hour ahead}, using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. The target is continuous pressure; attack labels would not be input features or the primary target. Node selection would follow a review of the network and available pressure measurements.\r\n\r\n\\textbf{Why consider it?}\r\nThe combination of hydraulic measurements and actuator states supports studying how network operation relates to future pressure. Its size is manageable and considerably exceeds its feature count. Existing attack-detection work provides background for a different focus on pressure forecasting, potentially followed by analysis under abnormal conditions. Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.\r\n\r\n\\vspace{3pt}\r\n\\textbf{Scope.}\r\nThese are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\r\n\\end{document}\r\nC:/Program Files/MiKTeX/tex/latex/tcolorbox/tcolorbox.sty\r\nkpsewhich: major issue: So far, you have not checked for updates as a MiKTeX user.\r\nkpsewhichC:/Program Files/MiKTeX/tex/latex/tools/tabularx.sty\r\n: major issue: So far, you have not checked for updates as a MiKTeX user.\r\nC:/Users/Andres/AppData/Roaming/MiKTeX/tex/latex/enumitem/enumitem.sty\r\nkpsewhich: major issue: So far, you have not checked for updates as a MiKTeX user.\r\n"

Arango-Martinez}\\par\n{\\Large\\bfseries Milestone 1: Proposed Datasets}\\par\n{\\normalsize Pressure Prediction in Drinking-Water Distribution Systems}\\par\n\\vspace{2pt}\\hrule\\vspace{5pt}\n\n\\textbf{Area of interest.}\nMy interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This builds on my previous work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements. For this project, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure. Both candidates below contain simulated network data and support a comparison of learning methods beyond exploratory analysis.\n\n\\datasetheading{1. BattLeDIM: L-Town}\n\\textbf{Sources:}\n\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle dataset}\n\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation (Zenodo)}.\n\n\\textbf{Description.}\nBattLeDIM was developed to evaluate leakage detection and localization in the simulated L-Town network. It provides measurements for 2018 and 2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 time observations per year, or 210,240 in total. Its 119 measurement channels comprise 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings of consumption. These are numerical time-series variables. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available.\n\n\\textbf{Proposed task.}\nPredict pressure at a selected monitored node \\textbf{15 minutes ahead}. Potential inputs include current and past pressures, flows, tank level, consumption, and time information. The target is a continuous pressure value, so this is regression rather than classification. The node would be selected after reviewing network topology and pressure conditions, with preference for a location relevant to critical-node pressure management.\n\n\\textbf{Why consider it?}\nIts measurement frequency and distributed sensors closely match my engineering interests, and it has substantially more observations than variables. The original benchmark offers documented prior work on leakage detection; pressure forecasting provides a different project focus. A possible later extension is to examine whether forecast errors change during leakage events.\n\n\\datasetheading{2. BATADAL: C-Town}\n\\textbf{Sources:}\n\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle dataset}\n\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n\\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation}.\n\n\\textbf{Description.}\nBATADAL was developed for cyberattack detection in the simulated C-Town network. The hourly data include 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 records in a second development set, and 2,089 test records: 15,027 in total. Its 43 SCADA variables comprise seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 actuator states, and 12 pressures. Measurements are numerical; actuator states are discrete. Attack information is available separately, with incomplete labeling in the original development data.\n\n\\textbf{Proposed task.}\nPredict pressure at a selected monitored network location \\textbf{one hour ahead}, using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. The target is continuous pressure; attack labels would not be input features or the primary target. Node selection would follow a review of the network and available pressure measurements.\n\n\\textbf{Why consider it?}\nThe combination of hydraulic measurements and actuator states supports studying how network operation relates to future pressure. Its size is manageable and considerably exceeds its feature count. Existing attack-detection work provides background for a different focus on pressure forecasting, potentially followed by analysis under abnormal conditions. Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.\n\n\\vspace{3pt}\n\\textbf{Scope.}\nThese are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\n\\end{document}\n"

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: D:/GitHub/ML NJIT/Kaggle/Proyecto/Milestone1.tex\n+\\documentclass[11pt,letterpaper]{article}\n+\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n+\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n+\\usepackage[margin=0.75in]{geometry}\n+\\usepackage{lmodern}\n+\\usepackage{xcolor}\n+\\usepackage{microtype}\n+\\usepackage{hyperref}\n+\\hypersetup{colorlinks=true,urlcolor=blue!45!black,\n+  pdftitle={DS675 Milestone 1 - Proposed Datasets},\n+  pdfauthor={A. Arango-Martinez}}\n+\\setlength{\\parindent}{0pt}\n+\\setlength{\\parskip}{5pt}\n+\\pagestyle{empty}\n+\\newcommand{\\datasetheading}[1]{\\vspace{5pt}\\textbf{\\large #1}\\par}\n+\n+\\begin{document}\n+{\\small DS675 \\hfill A. Arango-Martinez}\\par\n+{\\Large\\bfseries Milestone 1: Proposed Datasets}\\par\n+{\\normalsize Pressure Prediction in Drinking-Water Distribution Systems}\\par\n+\\vspace{2pt}\\hrule\\vspace{5pt}\n+\n+\\textbf{Area of interest.}\n+My interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This builds on my previous work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements. For this project, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure. Both candidates below contain simulated network data and support a comparison of learning methods beyond exploratory analysis.\n+\n+\\datasetheading{1. BattLeDIM: L-Town}\n+\\textbf{Sources:}\n+\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle dataset}\n+\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n+\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation (Zenodo)}.\n+\n+\\textbf{Description.}\n+BattLeDIM was developed to evaluate leakage detection and localization in the simulated L-Town network. It provides measurements for 2018 and 2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 time observations per year, or 210,240 in total. Its 119 measurement channels comprise 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings of consumption. These are numerical time-series variables. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available.\n+\n+\\textbf{Proposed task.}\n+Predict pressure at a selected monitored node \\textbf{15 minutes ahead}. Potential inputs include current and past pressures, flows, tank level, consumption, and time information. The target is a continuous pressure value, so this is regression rather than classification. The node would be selected after reviewing network topology and pressure conditions, with preference for a location relevant to critical-node pressure management.\n+\n+\\textbf{Why consider it?}\n+Its measurement frequency and distributed sensors closely match my engineering interests, and it has substantially more observations than variables. The original benchmark offers documented prior work on leakage detection; pressure forecasting provides a different project focus. A possible later extension is to examine whether forecast errors change during leakage events.\n+\n+\\datasetheading{2. BATADAL: C-Town}\n+\\textbf{Sources:}\n+\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle dataset}\n+\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n+\\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation}.\n+\n+\\textbf{Description.}\n+BATADAL was developed for cyberattack detection in the simulated C-Town network. The hourly data include 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 records in a second development set, and 2,089 test records: 15,027 in total. Its 43 SCADA variables comprise seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 actuator states, and 12 pressures. Measurements are numerical; actuator states are discrete. Attack information is available separately, with incomplete labeling in the original development data.\n+\n+\\textbf{Proposed task.}\n+Predict pressure at a selected monitored network location \\textbf{one hour ahead}, using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. The target is continuous pressure; attack labels would not be input features or the primary target. Node selection would follow a review of the network and available pressure measurements.\n+\n+\\textbf{Why consider it?}\n+The combination of hydraulic measurements and actuator states supports studying how network operation relates to future pressure. Its size is manageable and considerably exceeds its feature count. Existing attack-detection work provides background for a different focus on pressure forecasting, potentially followed by analysis under abnormal conditions. Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.\n+\n+\\vspace{3pt}\n+\\textbf{Scope.}\n+These are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\n+\\end{document}\n*** End Patch"));
```

### stdout

````text
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.

\vspace{1mm}
\begin{contextbox}
  \labeltext{Area of interest.}
  I am interested in predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This direction builds on my published work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements: \href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\textit{Dynamic Pressure Regulation in Water Distribution Systems through Remote Critical Node Control: A Case Study}}. For DS675, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure.
\end{contextbox}

\vspace{1.8mm}
{\color{Navy}\bfseries\large Candidates at a glance}\par
\vspace{1mm}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\bfseries\color{Navy}}p{1.1in}X X@{}}
  \rowcolor{Blue!13}
  & \textbf{BattLeDIM: L-Town} & \textbf{BATADAL: C-Town} \\
  Sampling & 5 minutes & 1 hour \\
  Scale & 210,240 time observations & 15,027 records \\
  Main data & Pressure, flow, tank level, demand & Pressure, flow, tank levels, actuator states \\
  Proposed target & Node pressure 15 minutes ahead & Node pressure 1 hour ahead \\
\end{tabularx}

\vspace{2mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Teal]{Candidate 1 \quad BattLeDIM: L-Town}
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & Simulated L-Town network data for 2018 and 2019. It contains 33 pressure sensors, three flow sensors, one tank-level sensor, and 82 automated meter readings: 119 numerical time-series channels. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available. \\
    \labeltext{ML task} & Predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead from current and past pressure, flow, tank level, consumption, and time information. The continuous target defines a regression task. \\
    \labeltext{Why it fits} & The five-minute interval and distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. Its original purpose is leak detection; pressure forecasting provides a different predictive use, with leakage periods available for later robustness analysis. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle} \enspace\textbar\enspace \href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

\vspace{1.5mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Gold]{Candidate 2 \quad BATADAL: C-Town}
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & Simulated C-Town network data recorded hourly. Its 43 SCADA variables include seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states, and 12 pressures. The files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 development records, and 2,089 test records. \\
    \labeltext{ML task} & Predict pressure at a selected monitored location one hour ahead from current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. Attack labels would not be inputs or the primary target. \\
    \labeltext{Why it fits} & Hydraulic measurements and actuator states allow study of how network operation relates to future pressure. Its original purpose is attack detection; pressure forecasting offers a separate supervised task, with abnormal periods available for later robustness analysis. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle} \enspace\textbar\enspace \href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.\r\n- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt=\"Complete request for that action\"}`.\r\n\\documentclass[10pt,letterpaper]{article}\r\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\r\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\r\n\\usepackage[left=0.62in,right=0.62in,top=0.42in,bottom=0.38in]{geometry}\r\n\\usepackage{lmodern}\r\n\\usepackage[table]{xcolor}\r\n\\usepackage{microtype}\r\n\\usepackage{tabularx}\r\n\\usepackage{array}\r\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\r\n\\usepackage[most]{tcolorbox}\r\n\\usepackage{hyperref}\r\n\r\n\\definecolor{Navy}{HTML}{12324A}\r\n\\definecolor{Blue}{HTML}{16697A}\r\n\\definecolor{Teal}{HTML}{2A9D8F}\r\n\\definecolor{PaleBlue}{HTML}{EDF5F7}\r\n\\definecolor{PaleGold}{HTML}{FFF7E7}\r\n\\definecolor{Gold}{HTML}{E9A23B}\r\n\\definecolor{Muted}{HTML}{5F6F79}\r\n\r\n\\hypersetup{\r\n  colorlinks=true,\r\n  urlcolor=Blue,\r\n  linkcolor=Blue,\r\n  pdftitle={DS675 Milestone 1 - Proposed Datasets},\r\n  pdfauthor={A. Arango-Martinez}\r\n  \\end{minipage}\r\n\\end{tcolorbox}\r\n\r\n\\vspace{1mm}\r\n\\begin{contextbox}\r\n  \\labeltext{Area of interest.}\r\n  I am interested in predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This direction builds on my published work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements: \\href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\\textit{Dynamic Pressure Regulation in Water Distribution Systems through Remote Critical Node Control: A Case Study}}. For DS675, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure.\r\n\\end{contextbox}\r\n\r\n\\vspace{1.8mm}\r\n{\\color{Navy}\\bfseries\\large Candidates at a glance}\\par\r\n\\vspace{1mm}\r\n\\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}\r\n\\begin{tabularx}{\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\bfseries\\color{Navy}}p{1.1in}X X@{}}\r\n  \\rowcolor{Blue!13}\r\n  & \\textbf{BattLeDIM: L-Town} & \\textbf{BATADAL: C-Town} \\\\\r\n  Sampling & 5 minutes & 1 hour \\\\\r\n  Scale & 210,240 time observations & 15,027 records \\\\\r\n  Main data & Pressure, flow, tank level, demand & Pressure, flow, tank levels, actuator states \\\\\r\n  Proposed target & Node pressure 15 minutes ahead & Node pressure 1 hour ahead \\\\\r\n\\end{tabularx}\r\n\r\n\\vspace{2mm}\r\n\\begin{datasetbox}[Teal]{Candidate 1 \\quad BattLeDIM: L-Town}\r\n  \\begin{tabularx}{\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}\r\n    \\labeltext{Dataset} & Simulated L-Town network data for 2018 and 2019. It contains 33 pressure sensors, three flow sensors, one tank-level sensor, and 82 automated meter readings: 119 numerical time-series channels. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{ML task} & Predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead from current and past pressure, flow, tank level, consumption, and time information. The continuous target defines a regression task. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{Why it fits} & The five-minute interval and distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. Its original purpose is leak detection; pressure forecasting provides a different predictive use, with leakage periods available for later robustness analysis. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{Sources} & \\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle} \\enspace\\textbar\\enspace \\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation} \\\\\r\n  \\end{tabularx}\r\n\\end{datasetbox}\r\n\r\n\\vspace{1.5mm}\r\n\\begin{datasetbox}[Gold]{Candidate 2 \\quad BATADAL: C-Town}\r\n  \\begin{tabularx}{\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\raggedright\\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}\r\n    \\labeltext{Dataset} & Simulated C-Town network data recorded hourly. Its 43 SCADA variables include seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states, and 12 pressures. The files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 development records, and 2,089 test records. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{ML task} & Predict pressure at a selected monitored location one hour ahead from current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. Attack labels would not be inputs or the primary target. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{Why it fits} & Hydraulic measurements and actuator states allow study of how network operation relates to future pressure. Its original purpose is attack detection; pressure forecasting offers a separate supervised task, with abnormal periods available for later robustness analysis. \\\\\r\n    \\labeltext{Sources} & \\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle} \\enspace\\textbar\\enspace \\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation} \\\\\r\n  \\end{tabularx}\r\n\\end{datasetbox}\r\n\r\n\\vfill\r\n\\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=PaleGold,colframe=Gold!75!black,boxrule=0.5pt,arc=1.5mm,\r\n  left=3mm,right=3mm,top=1.8mm,bottom=1.8mm]\r\n  \\small\\labeltext{Scope for this milestone.}\r\n  These are two candidate datasets; the final selection remains open. Detailed feature selection and model comparison will be developed in later milestones. Evaluation will preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\r\n\\end{tcolorbox}\r\n\r\n\\end{document}\r\n"
  }
]

Its original focus is attack detection, while my focus would be pressure forecasting and, later, model behavior during abnormal periods. \\\\\n    \\labeltext{Sources} & \\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle} \\enspace\\textbar\\enspace \\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation} \\\\\n  \\end{tabularx}\n\\end{datasetbox}\n\n\\vfill\n\\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=PaleGold,colframe=Gold!75!black,boxrule=0.5pt,arc=1.5mm,\n  left=3mm,right=3mm,top=1.8mm,bottom=1.8mm]\n  \\small\\labeltext{Scope for this milestone.}\n  At this stage, I am keeping both datasets as candidates. I will make the final selection after reviewing the network variables in more detail and considering instructor feedback. Feature selection and model comparison will be addressed in later milestones.\n\\end{tcolorbox}\n\n\\end{document}\n"

````text
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.

\vspace{1mm}
\begin{contextbox}
  \labeltext{Area of interest.}
  My main interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This question is closely connected to my professional experience and to my publication, \href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\textit{Dynamic Pressure Regulation in Water Distribution Systems through Remote Critical Node Control: A Case Study}}. In this project, I would like to explore whether supervised learning can use current and past SCADA measurements to make useful predictions of future pressure.
\end{contextbox}

\vspace{1.8mm}
{\color{Navy}\bfseries\large Candidates at a glance}\par
\vspace{1mm}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\bfseries\color{Navy}}p{1.1in}X X@{}}
  \rowcolor{Blue!13}
  & \textbf{BattLeDIM: L-Town} & \textbf{BATADAL: C-Town} \\
  Sampling & 5 minutes & 1 hour \\
  Scale & 210,240 time observations & 15,027 records \\
  Main data & 119 measurement channels (full inventory on page 2) & 43 SCADA variables (full inventory on page 2) \\
  Proposed target & Node pressure 15 minutes ahead & Node pressure 1 hour ahead \\
\end{tabularx}

\vspace{2mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Teal]{Candidate 1 \quad BattLeDIM: L-Town}
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & BattLeDIM represents the simulated L-Town network during 2018 and 2019. Its main data contain 119 numerical time-series channels: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings. Every feature is listed on page 2. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available. \\
    \labeltext{My proposed task} & I would predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead using current and past pressure, flow, tank level, consumption, and time information. Since the target is a continuous pressure value, this would be a regression problem. \\
    \labeltext{Why I chose it} & This dataset is appealing because its five-minute sampling and distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. It was created for leak detection, so pressure forecasting gives me a different predictive question while retaining leakage periods for a possible robustness study. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle} \enspace\textbar\enspace \href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

\vspace{1.5mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Gold]{Candidate 2 \quad BATADAL: C-Town}
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & BATADAL represents the simulated C-Town network at hourly intervals. Its main data contain 43 SCADA variables: seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states, and 12 pressures. Every feature is listed on page 2. The files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 development records, and 2,089 test records. \\
    \labeltext{My proposed task} & I would predict pressure at a selected monitored location one hour ahead using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. Attack labels would not be inputs or the main target. \\
    \labeltext{Why I chose it} & I am interested in BATADAL because it combines hydraulic measurements with actuator states, which makes it possible to study how system operation relates to future pressure. Its original focus is attack detection, while my focus would be pressure forecasting and, later, model behavior during abnormal periods. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle} \enspace\textbar\enspace \href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

  enhanced, colback=Navy, colframe=Navy, arc=2mm, boxrule=0pt,

WR-WRENG-7321_AUTHOR_CORRECTIONS (1).pdf 4105253
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.

Area of interest. My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water distribution systems, particularly pressure
behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods,
as described in a published case study. For this project, I would like to approach a related problem from a machine-learning perspective:
whether supervised learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to predict future pressure at a monitored location.
Dataset selection rationale. I selected BattLeDIM and BATADAL not only because copies are available on Kaggle, but because
both are established research benchmarks with identifiable official sources. Their repositories provide network models, event information,
benchmark documentation, reproducibility resources, and peer-reviewed studies. This lets me verify the data independently of Kaggle
and trace previous work, which will be useful in Milestone 2 for identifying a predictive task that extends rather than simply repeats
prior results.
Candidates at a glance
BattLeDIM: L-Town
BATADAL: C-Town

Candidate 1
BattLeDIM: L-Town
Dataset
BattLeDIM is an academic benchmark for the virtual L-Town drinking-water distribution network. Its 2018–2019
SCADA data contain 119 numerical channels: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter
readings. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available; all main features are listed on page 2.
My proposed
task
I would predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead using current and past pressure, flow, tank
level, consumption, and time information. Since the target is a continuous pressure value, this would be a regression
problem.
Why I chose it
Its five-minute distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. Beyond Kaggle,
the official Zenodo archive provides a DOI, network models and event data, while the project site links evaluation
resources, the benchmark paper, and a special collection of related studies. This makes provenance and prior work
traceable. I would study pressure forecasting rather than repeat the original leakage-detection task, with leakage
periods retained for a possible robustness analysis.
Sources
Kaggle copy | Official Zenodo dataset | Benchmark site | Benchmark paper
Candidate 2
BATADAL: C-Town
Dataset
BATADAL is a documented benchmark for the simulated C-Town water distribution network. Its hourly SCADA
data contain 43 variables: seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states, and 12 pressures. The files contain
8,761 normal-operation, 4,177 development, and 2,089 test records; all main features are listed on page 2.
My proposed
task
I would predict pressure at a selected monitored location one hour ahead using current and past tank levels, pump
and valve states, flows, and pressures. Attack labels would not be inputs or the main target.
Why I chose it
BATADAL was selected because it combines hydraulic measurements with explicit pump and valve states, allowing
investigation of how system operation relates to future pressure. Beyond the Kaggle copy, the official benchmark
site provides the original datasets, attack-event records, the C-Town EPANET model, challenge rules, and the
peer-reviewed paper documenting the benchmark and submitted approaches. These resources make the dataset
independently verifiable and make previous work traceable, which is valuable for identifying a distinct supervised
learning task. The proposed project would focus on pressure forecasting rather than repeat attack detection, while
attack periods could later support a robustness test.
Sources
Kaggle copy | Official datasets | Benchmark materials | Benchmark paper
